People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’ve just been feeling a bit too busy inside, if that makes sense. Even on a quiet day, I’m up and moving from one thing to another — the garden club list, then my knitting bag, then some travel papers — and I realize I haven’t really finished any of them. It’s not terrible, I don’t mean to make a fuss, but it’s been enough that I thought maybe I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I worry about the usual things, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time. At least, I don’t think so. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I hate to make it sound worse than it is. When I help with the community center papers, I might miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I’ll put the wrong room time on a note even though I really thought I’d checked it. It’s usually small things, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. If I’m sitting still too long, I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something. It’s not awful, but it does make it hard to just sit quietly sometimes. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these feelings of restlessness and being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh… probably much earlier than I used to admit. Even in grade school, I remember my teachers telling my mother that I was bright, but sort of scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I was supposed to, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. I don’t think I was trying to be difficult… but yes, it was there pretty young, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well… not in a reckless way, I don’t think. But I do sometimes jump in before I’ve really thought it through. My husband says I cut him off before he finishes talking, and I suppose he’s right. I do it in group planning too sometimes, because if I don’t say the thought right then, I’m afraid I’ll lose it. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or even just watching television, I’ll usually be tapping my foot a little, or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap, like a napkin or my purse strap. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense emotional reactions to things that happen to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense, exactly. I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I usually try to keep it to myself and not make a scene. Sometimes things bother me more than I think they should, maybe… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life, such as problems at work or issues with personal relationships, because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t work anymore, so it’s mostly at home and at the community center. I try very hard to keep things straight, but lately my husband and some of the other volunteers have had to check behind me with plans, and I can tell it’s been causing some strain. I don’t want to be unreliable, you know… but sometimes they just can’t quite count on me unless someone double-checks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’ve just been feeling a bit too busy inside, if that makes sense. Even on a quiet day, I’m up and moving from one thing to another — the garden club list, then my knitting bag, then some travel papers — and I realize I haven’t really finished any of them. It’s not terrible, I don’t mean to make a fuss, but it’s been enough that I thought maybe I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I worry about the usual things, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time. At least, I don’t think so. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I hate to make it sound worse than it is. When I help with the community center papers, I might miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I’ll put the wrong room time on a note even though I really thought I’d checked it. It’s usually small things, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. If I’m sitting still too long, I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something. It’s not awful, but it does make it hard to just sit quietly sometimes. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these feelings of restlessness and being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh… probably much earlier than I used to admit. Even in grade school, I remember my teachers telling my mother that I was bright, but sort of scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I was supposed to, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. I don’t think I was trying to be difficult… but yes, it was there pretty young, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well… not in a reckless way, I don’t think. But I do sometimes jump in before I’ve really thought it through. My husband says I cut him off before he finishes talking, and I suppose he’s right. I do it in group planning too sometimes, because if I don’t say the thought right then, I’m afraid I’ll lose it. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or even just watching television, I’ll usually be tapping my foot a little, or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap, like a napkin or my purse strap. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense emotional reactions to things that happen to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense, exactly. I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I usually try to keep it to myself and not make a scene. Sometimes things bother me more than I think they should, maybe… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life, such as problems at work or issues with personal relationships, because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t work anymore, so it’s mostly at home and at the community center. I try very hard to keep things straight, but lately my husband and some of the other volunteers have had to check behind me with plans, and I can tell it’s been causing some strain. I don’t want to be unreliable, you know… but sometimes they just can’t quite count on me unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty with completing tasks or your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t like saying it that way. At home it’s caused some tension, because my husband has to remind me or check what I said I’d do. And at the community center, the other volunteers have started double-checking plans with me, which is fair, I suppose, but it makes me feel bad. I don’t mean to be difficult… it’s just gotten to where they can’t always count on me to keep everything straight on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a significant activity because of your difficulties with attention or restlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t quit anything because of it… not exactly. I’m still helping at the community center. But I will say it’s started to cause some real strain, both there and at home, because my husband and the other volunteers don’t seem able to count on me to keep the plans straight unless someone checks behind me. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s probably the honest answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I hate to make it sound worse than it is. When I help with the community center papers, I might miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I’ll put the wrong room time on a note even though I really thought I’d checked it. It’s usually small things, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. If I’m sitting still too long, I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something. It’s not awful, but it does make it hard to just sit quietly sometimes. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these feelings of restlessness and being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh… probably much earlier than I used to admit. Even in grade school, I remember my teachers telling my mother that I was bright, but sort of scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I was supposed to, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. I don’t think I was trying to be difficult… but yes, it was there pretty young, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well… not in a reckless way, I don’t think. But I do sometimes jump in before I’ve really thought it through. My husband says I cut him off before he finishes talking, and I suppose he’s right. I do it in group planning too sometimes, because if I don’t say the thought right then, I’m afraid I’ll lose it. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or even just watching television, I’ll usually be tapping my foot a little, or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap, like a napkin or my purse strap. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense emotional reactions to things that happen to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense, exactly. I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I usually try to keep it to myself and not make a scene. Sometimes things bother me more than I think they should, maybe… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life, such as problems at work or issues with personal relationships, because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t work anymore, so it’s mostly at home and at the community center. I try very hard to keep things straight, but lately my husband and some of the other volunteers have had to check behind me with plans, and I can tell it’s been causing some strain. I don’t want to be unreliable, you know… but sometimes they just can’t quite count on me unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty with completing tasks or your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t like saying it that way. At home it’s caused some tension, because my husband has to remind me or check what I said I’d do. And at the community center, the other volunteers have started double-checking plans with me, which is fair, I suppose, but it makes me feel bad. I don’t mean to be difficult… it’s just gotten to where they can’t always count on me to keep everything straight on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a significant activity because of your difficulties with attention or restlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t quit anything because of it… not exactly. I’m still helping at the community center. But I will say it’s started to cause some real strain, both there and at home, because my husband and the other volunteers don’t seem able to count on me to keep the plans straight unless someone checks behind me. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in social, occupational, or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that’s been upsetting. I feel bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it’s been my whole adult life, exactly. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I suppose, but it’s really been especially noticeable for… maybe about a year and ten months now, since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center on top of everything else. That’s when it started feeling harder to keep up with. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine if symptoms meet criteria for onset during childhood", "question": "Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as restlessness or difficulty paying attention, before you were 12 years old?"}

I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I usually try to keep it to myself and not make a scene. Sometimes things bother me more than I think they should, maybe… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life, such as problems at work or issues with personal relationships, because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t work anymore, so it’s mostly at home and at the community center. I try very hard to keep things straight, but lately my husband and some of the other volunteers have had to check behind me with plans, and I can tell it’s been causing some strain. I don’t want to be unreliable, you know… but sometimes they just can’t quite count on me unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty with completing tasks or your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t like saying it that way. At home it’s caused some tension, because my husband has to remind me or check what I said I’d do. And at the community center, the other volunteers have started double-checking plans with me, which is fair, I suppose, but it makes me feel bad. I don’t mean to be difficult… it’s just gotten to where they can’t always count on me to keep everything straight on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a significant activity because of your difficulties with attention or restlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t quit anything because of it… not exactly. I’m still helping at the community center. But I will say it’s started to cause some real strain, both there and at home, because my husband and the other volunteers don’t seem able to count on me to keep the plans straight unless someone checks behind me. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in social, occupational, or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that’s been upsetting. I feel bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it’s been my whole adult life, exactly. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I suppose, but it’s really been especially noticeable for… maybe about a year and ten months now, since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center on top of everything else. That’s when it started feeling harder to keep up with. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as restlessness or difficulty paying attention, before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just sort of… scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I should, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. That kind of thing. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’ve just been feeling a bit too busy inside, if that makes sense. Even on a quiet day, I’m up and moving from one thing to another — the garden club list, then my knitting bag, then some travel papers — and I realize I haven’t really finished any of them. It’s not terrible, I don’t mean to make a fuss, but it’s been enough that I thought maybe I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I worry about the usual things, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time. At least, I don’t think so. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I hate to make it sound worse than it is. When I help with the community center papers, I might miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I’ll put the wrong room time on a note even though I really thought I’d checked it. But I will say it’s started to cause some real strain, both there and at home, because my husband and the other volunteers don’t seem able to count on me to keep the plans straight unless someone checks behind me. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in social, occupational, or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that’s been upsetting. I feel bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it’s been my whole adult life, exactly. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I suppose, but it’s really been especially noticeable for… maybe about a year and ten months now, since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center on top of everything else. That’s when it started feeling harder to keep up with. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as restlessness or difficulty paying attention, before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just sort of… scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I should, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. That kind of thing. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. It’s not as if everything is falling apart, but it has started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband, and some of the other volunteers, can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone double-checks behind me. I know that’s frustrating for them, and I feel badly about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’ve just been feeling a bit too busy inside, if that makes sense. Even on a quiet day, I’m up and moving from one thing to another — the garden club list, then my knitting bag, then some travel papers — and I realize I haven’t really finished any of them. It’s not terrible, I don’t mean to make a fuss, but it’s been enough that I thought maybe I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I worry about the usual things, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time. At least, I don’t think so. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I hate to make it sound worse than it is. When I help with the community center papers, I might miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I’ll put the wrong room time on a note even though I really thought I’d checked it. It’s usually small things, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. If I’m sitting still too long, I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something. It’s not awful, but it does make it hard to just sit quietly sometimes. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these feelings of restlessness and being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh… probably much earlier than I used to admit. Even in grade school, I remember my teachers telling my mother that I was bright, but sort of scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I was supposed to, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. I don’t think I was trying to be difficult… but yes, it was there pretty young, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well… not in a reckless way, I don’t think. But I do sometimes jump in before I’ve really thought it through. My husband says I cut him off before he finishes talking, and I suppose he’s right. I do it in group planning too sometimes, because if I don’t say the thought right then, I’m afraid I’ll lose it. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or even just watching television, I’ll usually be tapping my foot a little, or rubbing my fingers together. I don’t want to be unreliable, you know… but sometimes they just can’t quite count on me unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty with completing tasks or your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t like saying it that way. It’s just started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that’s been upsetting. I feel bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it’s been my whole adult life, exactly. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I suppose, but it’s really been especially noticeable for… maybe about a year and ten months now, since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center on top of everything else. That’s when it started feeling harder to keep up with. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as restlessness or difficulty paying attention, before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just sort of… scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I should, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. That kind of thing. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. It’s not as if everything is falling apart, but it has started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband, and some of the other volunteers, can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone double-checks behind me. I know that’s frustrating for them, and I feel badly about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity-impulsivity for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been pretty noticeable for about a year and ten months now—since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center along with my other activities. So… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’ve just been feeling a bit too busy inside, if that makes sense. Even on a quiet day, I’m up and moving from one thing to another — the garden club list, then my knitting bag, then some travel papers — and I realize I haven’t really finished any of them. It’s not terrible, I don’t mean to make a fuss, but it’s been enough that I thought maybe I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I worry about the usual things, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time. At least, I don’t think so. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I hate to make it sound worse than it is. When I help with the community center papers, I might miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I’ll put the wrong room time on a note even though I really thought I’d checked it. It’s usually small things, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. If I’m sitting still too long, I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something. It’s not awful, but it does make it hard to just sit quietly sometimes. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these feelings of restlessness and being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh… probably much earlier than I used to admit. Even in grade school, I remember my teachers telling my mother that I was bright, but sort of scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I was supposed to, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. I don’t think I was trying to be difficult… but yes, it was there pretty young, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well… not in a reckless way, I don’t think. But I do sometimes jump in before I’ve really thought it through. My husband says I cut him off before he finishes talking, and I suppose he’s right. I do it in group planning too sometimes, because if I don’t say the thought right then, I’m afraid I’ll lose it. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or even just watching television, I’ll usually be tapping my foot a little, or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap, like a napkin or my purse strap. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense emotional reactions to things that happen to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense, exactly. I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I usually try to keep it to myself and not make a scene. Sometimes things bother me more than I think they should, maybe… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life, such as problems at work or issues with personal relationships, because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t work anymore, so it’s mostly at home and at the community center. I try very hard to keep things straight, but lately my husband and some of the other volunteers have had to check behind me with plans, and I can tell it’s been causing some strain. I don’t want to be unreliable, you know… but sometimes they just can’t quite count on me unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty with completing tasks or your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t like saying it that way. My husband and the other volunteers can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that’s been upsetting. I feel bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it’s been my whole adult life, exactly. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I suppose, but it’s really been especially noticeable for… maybe about a year and ten months now, since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center on top of everything else. That’s when it started feeling harder to keep up with. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as restlessness or difficulty paying attention, before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just sort of… scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I should, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. That kind of thing. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. It’s not as if everything is falling apart, but it has started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband, and some of the other volunteers, can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone double-checks behind me. I know that’s frustrating for them, and I feel badly about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity-impulsivity for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been pretty noticeable for about a year and ten months now—since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center along with my other activities. So… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think I’ve lost friends over it or anything like that, but it does create strain. Mostly at the community center, with the other volunteers… and at home with my husband too. They can’t always count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and I know that can be frustrating. I hate admitting that, but yes, it has caused some problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’ve just been feeling a bit too busy inside, if that makes sense. Even on a quiet day, I’m up and moving from one thing to another — the garden club list, then my knitting bag, then some travel papers — and I realize I haven’t really finished any of them. It’s not terrible, I don’t mean to make a fuss, but it’s been enough that I thought maybe I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious about things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a big way. I mean, I worry about the usual things, I suppose, but I wouldn’t say I’m anxious all the time. At least, I don’t think so. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I hate to make it sound worse than it is. When I help with the community center papers, I might miss a name on the sign-up sheet, or I’ll put the wrong room time on a note even though I really thought I’d checked it. It’s usually small things, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, a bit. If I’m sitting still too long, I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something. It’s not awful, but it does make it hard to just sit quietly sometimes. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these feelings of restlessness and being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh… probably much earlier than I used to admit. Even in grade school, I remember my teachers telling my mother that I was bright, but sort of scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I was supposed to, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. I don’t think I was trying to be difficult… but yes, it was there pretty young, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well… not in a reckless way, I don’t think. But I do sometimes jump in before I’ve really thought it through. My husband says I cut him off before he finishes talking, and I suppose he’s right. I do it in group planning too sometimes, because if I don’t say the thought right then, I’m afraid I’ll lose it. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or even just watching television, I’ll usually be tapping my foot a little, or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap, like a napkin or my purse strap. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense emotional reactions to things that happen to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense, exactly. I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I usually try to keep it to myself and not make a scene. Sometimes things bother me more than I think they should, maybe… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life, such as problems at work or issues with personal relationships, because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t work anymore, so it’s mostly at home and at the community center. I try very hard to keep things straight, but lately my husband and some of the other volunteers have had to check behind me with plans, and I can tell it’s been causing some strain. I don’t want to be unreliable, you know… but sometimes they just can’t quite count on me unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty with completing tasks or your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t like saying it that way. At home it’s caused some tension, because my husband has to remind me or check what I said I’d do. And at the community center, the other volunteers have started double-checking plans with me, which is fair, I suppose, but it makes me feel bad. I don’t mean to be difficult… it’s just gotten to where they can’t always count on me to keep everything straight on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a significant activity because of your difficulties with attention or restlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t quit anything because of it… not exactly. I’m still helping at the community center. But I will say it’s started to cause some real strain, both there and at home, because my husband and the other volunteers don’t seem able to count on me to keep the plans straight unless someone checks behind me. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in social, occupational, or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that’s been upsetting. I feel bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it’s been my whole adult life, exactly. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I suppose, but it’s really been especially noticeable for… maybe about a year and ten months now, since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center on top of everything else. That’s when it started feeling harder to keep up with. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as restlessness or difficulty paying attention, before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just sort of… scattered. My husband, and some of the other volunteers, can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone double-checks behind me. I know that’s frustrating for them, and I feel badly about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity-impulsivity for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say so. I hate admitting that, but yes, it has caused some problems.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are currently causing mild, moderate, or severe problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I would say mild… I think. It’s not ruining my life or anything like that, and I don’t want to overstate it. But it has started causing some real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t always count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, so… mild, but definitely noticeable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t mean to be difficult… it’s just gotten to where they can’t always count on me to keep everything straight on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a significant activity because of your difficulties with attention or restlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t quit anything because of it… not exactly. I’m still helping at the community center. But I will say it’s started to cause some real strain, both there and at home, because my husband and the other volunteers don’t seem able to count on me to keep the plans straight unless someone checks behind me. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in social, occupational, or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that’s been upsetting. I feel bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it’s been my whole adult life, exactly. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I suppose, but it’s really been especially noticeable for… maybe about a year and ten months now, since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center on top of everything else. That’s when it started feeling harder to keep up with. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as restlessness or difficulty paying attention, before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just sort of… scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I should, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. That kind of thing. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. It’s not as if everything is falling apart, but it has started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband, and some of the other volunteers, can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone double-checks behind me. I know that’s frustrating for them, and I feel badly about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity-impulsivity for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been pretty noticeable for about a year and ten months now—since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center along with my other activities. So… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think I’ve lost friends over it or anything like that, but it does create strain. Mostly at the community center, with the other volunteers… and at home with my husband too. They can’t always count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and I know that can be frustrating. I hate admitting that, but yes, it has caused some problems.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are currently causing mild, moderate, or severe problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I would say mild… I think. It’s not ruining my life or anything like that, and I don’t want to overstate it. But it has started causing some real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t always count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, so… mild, but definitely noticeable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. If I’m sitting still too long, I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside, like I ought to stand up and straighten something or go check on something. It’s not awful, but it does make it hard to just sit quietly sometimes. Is that the kind of restlessness you mean?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these feelings of restlessness and being easily distracted?\n\nPatient: Oh… probably much earlier than I used to admit. Even in grade school, I remember my teachers telling my mother that I was bright, but sort of scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I was supposed to, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. I don’t think I was trying to be difficult… but yes, it was there pretty young, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Well… not in a reckless way, I don’t think. But I do sometimes jump in before I’ve really thought it through. My husband says I cut him off before he finishes talking, and I suppose he’s right. I do it in group planning too sometimes, because if I don’t say the thought right then, I’m afraid I’ll lose it. I don’t mean to be rude, truly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet, or tapping on things, when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I hope it’s not too distracting to other people. If I’m sitting in a meeting, or even just watching television, I’ll usually be tapping my foot a little, or rubbing my fingers together. Sometimes I fuss with whatever’s in my lap, like a napkin or my purse strap. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense emotional reactions to things that happen to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them intense, exactly. I mean, I can get upset like anyone does, but I usually try to keep it to myself and not make a scene. Sometimes things bother me more than I think they should, maybe… but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your daily life, such as problems at work or issues with personal relationships, because of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I mean, I don’t work anymore, so it’s mostly at home and at the community center. I try very hard to keep things straight, but lately my husband and some of the other volunteers have had to check behind me with plans, and I can tell it’s been causing some strain. I don’t want to be unreliable, you know… but sometimes they just can’t quite count on me unless someone double-checks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any problems in your personal relationships due to your difficulty with completing tasks or your restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I don’t like saying it that way. At home it’s caused some tension, because my husband has to remind me or check what I said I’d do. And at the community center, the other volunteers have started double-checking plans with me, which is fair, I suppose, but it makes me feel bad. I don’t mean to be difficult… it’s just gotten to where they can’t always count on me to keep everything straight on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a volunteer position, or a significant activity because of your difficulties with attention or restlessness?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t quit anything because of it… not exactly. I’m still helping at the community center. But I will say it’s started to cause some real strain, both there and at home, because my husband and the other volunteers don’t seem able to count on me to keep the plans straight unless someone checks behind me. I don’t like admitting that, but that’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or problems in social, occupational, or other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that’s been upsetting. I feel bad about it, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty with attention consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it’s been my whole adult life, exactly. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I suppose, but it’s really been especially noticeable for… maybe about a year and ten months now, since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center on top of everything else. That’s when it started feeling harder to keep up with. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these symptoms, such as restlessness or difficulty paying attention, before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but even in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright, just sort of… scattered. I’d lose papers, talk before I should, and wiggle around in my seat before I’d finished my work. That kind of thing. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying it that way. It’s not as if everything is falling apart, but it has started causing real strain at home and at the community center. My husband, and some of the other volunteers, can’t really count on me to keep plans straight unless someone double-checks behind me. I know that’s frustrating for them, and I feel badly about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity-impulsivity for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say so. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it’s been pretty noticeable for about a year and ten months now—since I took on that extra coordinating at the community center along with my other activities. So… yes, definitely more than six months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think I’ve lost friends over it or anything like that, but it does create strain. Mostly at the community center, with the other volunteers… and at home with my husband too. They can’t always count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and I know that can be frustrating. I hate admitting that, but yes, it has caused some problems.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are currently causing mild, moderate, or severe problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I would say mild… I think. It’s not ruining my life or anything like that, and I don’t want to overstate it. But it has started causing some real strain at home and at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t always count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, so… mild, but definitely noticeable.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to make it sound too dramatic. It’s more that I feel embarrassed and upset when things get strained at home or at the community center. My husband and the other volunteers can’t always count on me to keep plans straight unless someone checks behind me, and that does bother me. So yes, it has caused me distress, just… maybe not severe distress."
